1     Localized recharge processes in the NE Mekong Delta and implications for groundwater quality 2 3     Tu T.A.1,2, Tweed S. 1,3*, Dan N.P.1,2, Descloitres M.1,4, Quang K.H. 1,2, Nemery J. 1,4, Nguyen A.1,4, 4     Leblanc M.1,5,6, Baduel C.1,4 5 1 6       Centre Asiatique de Recherche sur l'Eau (CARE), Ho Chi Minh City University of Technology 7     (HCMUT), Ho Chi Minh City 700000, Vietnam 2 8       Vietnam National University-Ho Chi Minh City (VNU-HCM), Thu Duc City, Ho Chi Minh City, Vietnam 3 9      UMR G-eau, IRD, SupAgro, Montpellier, France 4 10      Université Grenoble Alpes, CNRS, IRD, Grenoble INP, IGE, Grenoble, France 5 11      UMR EMMAH, Hydrogeology Laboratory, University of Avignon, France 6 12      IWRI (International Water Research Institute), UM6P-UM$_5$, Morocco 13 * 14         Corresponding author, [EMAIL_REDACTED] 15 16 17 18 19 20 21 [SOURCE: Page_1]

22 23   Abstract 24   Understanding recharge in the Mekong Delta is critical for the delta’s groundwater resources, and 25   requires the investigation of recharge processes at the local scale. In this study of the north eastern 26   area of the Mekong Delta, time-series of environmental tracer data (δ18O, δ2H, major ions and 3H) and 27   markers of rural pollution (NH$_4$ and NO$_3$) were used to highlight localized recharge and impacts on 28   groundwater quality. Results highlighted new hydrological insights into recharge processes, including 29   that the Pleistocene aquifer receives recent recharge (< 60 years), predominantly during high rainfall 30   months (> 100 mm/month). However, due to shallow clay layers there are significant spatial variations 31   in these recharge processes, which were observed in the seasonal fluctuation of groundwater δ18O 32   values in groundwater. Wet season δ18O changes ranged from below analytical uncertainty (≤ 0.10 ‰) 33   to up to 0.56 ‰, and the calculated fraction of rainfall contribution to the aquifer is ≤ 5% to 16%. 34   Rainfall recharge via the acrisol soils results in low groundwater EC (20-55 µS/cm), acidic groundwater 35   (pH 3.6-5.6), and may also have resulted in the low groundwater NO$_3$ concentrations (≤ 5.3 mg NO$_3$/L) 36   at many sites due to adsorption, therefore delaying not reducing NO$_3$ contamination. Site specific 37   variations in nitrogen processes includes increased NO$_3$ (to 29.7 mg/L) from fertiliser transfers or 38   nitrification, and increased NH$_4$ (to 1.4 mg/L) likely due to the recharge of irrigation waters. Unlike 39   other recharge areas across the northern Mekong Delta, this north-eastern region provides a 40   groundwater resource unaffected by arsenic contamination. Therefore, these results should inform on 41   priority areas for protection from further contamination by rural anthropogenic activities. 42 43 44 45   Key words 46   Localized recharge, groundwater, stable isotopes, tritium, nitrate, acidity. 47 [SOURCE: Page_2]

48   1. Introduction 49 50   The investigation of localized recharge processes has largely been documented in semi-arid regions. 51   Localized recharge processes have been found to result from many land surface or subsurface features, 52   including flow via sinkholes in calcrete layers in the Stampriet Basin (Kirchner et al., 2002), via localized 53   depressions focalising rainfall recharge in south-west Niger (e.g. Leduc et al., 2001; Seddon et al., 54   2021), and via preferential recharge pathways in dual porosity mediums in southeast Australia 55   (Cartwright et al., 2007) and in southern California (Manna et al., 2017). However, in more humid 56   climates, identifying localized recharge processes is also important for groundwater resources, both in 57   terms of groundwater resource quantity and quality (Hartmann et al., 2021). 58 59   Regional groundwater recharge rate assessments can mask significant variations at the local scale that 60   potentially play an important role in groundwater recharge. For example, previous work in the tropical 61   humid Mekong Delta found that regional estimates of groundwater recharge were underestimated 62   when localized recharge processes were not included (Minderhound et al., 2017; 2020). In addition, 63   there are often significant temporal and spatial heterogeneities associated with localized recharge 64   processes, which are critical when considering groundwater resource protection and contaminant 65   transfers. For example, the groundwater recharge for Ho Chi Minh City (HCMC), which is 66   predominantly from rainfall and river sources in the Saigon-Dong Nai river catchment basin (HCMC 67   Geography, 2020), is significantly impacted by land use and land cover change (Adhikari et al., 2020). 68 69   More than 55 million inhabitants of Vietnam (about 60 % of the total population) are groundwater- 70   dependant (Carrard et al., 2019). Many households that use groundwater as their primary source of 71   drinking water, which are mostly located in rural areas, have private shallow bores rather than sourcing 72   groundwater from government-managed plants (Carrard et al., 2019). Therefore, there remains an 73   elevated health risk for inhabitants where shallow aquifers are exposed to contamination. A majority 74   of the rural population in Vietnam lives in the Red River Delta and the Mekong River Delta where the 75   use of groundwater for domestic purposes dates back at least 100 years (Anderson, 1978; Luu, 2019). 76   In the Mekong Delta, the high groundwater extraction rates have resulted in declining groundwater 77   levels being observed in all aquifers (Ha et al., 2019b; Duy et al., 2021). Groundwater is exploited for 78   agricultural, industrial and domestic uses beyond the recharge capacity around HCMC. The over- 79   exploitation has resulted in land subsidence and salinity intrusion along the coast of the Mekong River 80   Delta (Stefan, 2014; Duong et al., 2010). 81 [SOURCE: Page_3]

82   Despite a heavy reliance on groundwater as a resource in the Mekong Delta, there still remains many 83   questions relating to localized recharge processes and implications for groundwater quality. In this 84   study we analyse the localized recharge processes in an area where the shallow aquifer belongs to the 85   northeastern margins of the Mekong Delta groundwater system, but the river forms part of Saigon- 86   (Dong Nai) river catchment basin. The seasonal variations in recharge processes, origins of water 87   recharging the aquifer, and impacts of recharge on water quality parameters are analysed using 88   environmental tracer data. The time-series of environmental tracer data (stable isotopes of water 89   (δ18O and δ2H), major ions and tritium (3H)) are used to determine whether recharge processes are 90   recent and localized, and therefore an assessment of whether the presence of suspected large shallow 91   clay deposits could act as confining layers for the shallow aquifer and protect (or not) the groundwater 92   resources from surface contaminants. The combined environmental tracers, pH, electrical conductivity 93   (EC), and nitrogen concentration data are also used to determine whether recharge processes affect 94   groundwater quality processes. 95 96 97   2. Study Area 98   The study site located in a rural area of HCMC, Vietnam (Fig. 1). The area is about 31 km to the 99   northwest of the city centre. The elevation varies from 0 to 25 m AMSL (above mean sea level). Near 100   the Saigon River, the elevation is lowest (< 2 m AMSL), and increases with distance from the river in 101   the north-easterly and south-westerly directions. The highest elevation in the north eastern and south 102   eastern sides are about 30 and 25 m AMSL, respectively. The area has a monsoon wet tropical climate, 103   with a rainy and a dry season resulting in significant seasonal changes in rainfall (e.g. Fig. 2a). Average 104   annual rainfall is around 1900 mm and monthly temperature varies from 27.5⁰C (September) to 29.5⁰C 105   (March) (GSO, 2016). 106 107   HCMC is the most important area for economic development and is the most populated area in 108   Vietnam (population up to 8.2 million people). The land use pattern in the area has rapidly changed 109   because of the economic development and urbanization during the last two decades (Nguyen et al., 110   2016; Son et al., 2017). In particular, urban areas have expanded 4.8-fold from 1990 to 2012 (Kontgis 111   et al., 2014). In 2015, the land of HCMC was used for urban (29.8%), agriculture (32.4 %) and forest 112   area (15.9%) (GSO, 2016). At the study site land use is mostly for agriculture. Soil in the study site is 113   mostly acrisol, forming through the deep weathering and extensive leaching of soluble components 114   due to high air temperatures and precipitation (Spaargaren and Deckers, 2005; FAO, 2015). The soil is 115   characterized by red and yellow appearance due to high contents of Fe-/Al-oxides/hydroxides, which 116   therefore has ferralic properties (Ha et al., 2019b). The soil quality has acidic pH (average ± standard [SOURCE: Page_4]

117   deviation, 4.72±0.4), low clay fraction (37±12.5%), low organic matter (<1.2) and low cation exchange 118   capacity (CEC, 4.7±1.67 meq/100g; % of total bases/sediment exchange capacity, 15±16%; Do et al., 119   2002). Quartz (~ 12%) and kaolinite or halloysite (~92%) are the most dominant minerals, and no calcite 120   is observed in the clay fraction of the soil (Nguyen et al., 2008). 121 122   The main river systems in the HCMC area are the Saigon and Dong Nai Rivers, which are connected to 123   two reservoirs, the Dau Tieng and Tri An Reservoirs in the upstream area of the Saigon River and Dong 124   Nai River respectively. The reservoirs were built to both store water for water supply and manage the 125   river water levels and salinization in the downstream areas in HCMC. Due to the low elevation, 126   seawater can strongly influence HCMC, causing flood and salinization in the area (Vachaud et al., 2019). 127 128   The geology is characterized by unconsolidated sediments of Miocene to Holocene overlying Mesozoic 129   to Paleozoic bedrocks (SVGMD, 2004). The unconsolidated sediment layer is progressively thicker from 130   north to south, and the thickness is up to 300 m in the south of HCMC. The Holocene sediment covers 131   low land area with elevations less than 5 m AMSL. Older sediment outcrops in the area where the 132   surface elevation is generally greater than 2 m AMSL. The outcrops of Pleistocene sediment are 133   characterized by a highly permeable laterite layer. Therefore, the high elevation areas are recognized 134   as a recharge zone of the aquifer systems (Ha et al., 2019b). At the study site, Holocene sediment 135   deposits along the Saigon River overly the upper Pleistocene sediment. The Pleistocene sediment 136   outcrops in high elevation areas on both sides of the Saigon River. Below the Pleistocene sediment is 137   the sediment of the middle Pliocene. The lower Pliocene sediment is the deepest layer in the area. 138   According to the Southern Vietnam Geological Mapping Division (SVGMD, 2004), the aquifer systems 139   in the HCMC can be divided into 7 aquifers including the Holocene (qh), upper Pleistocene (qp^3), upper- 140   middle Pleistocene (qp^2-3), lower Pleistocene (qp^1), middle Pliocene (n^22), lower Pliocene (n^21). Each 141   system comprises two parts including low permeability silt, clay, or silty clay layers that act as 142   aquitards, and the underlying aquifer that consists of fine to coarse sand (Fig. 1c). One of the major 143   questions in this study is to determine whether the shallow clays acts as a continuous confining layer 144   and therefore limits local recharge, or whether the clays are present as discontinuous lenses and so 145   allows spatially heterogeneous recharge to occur and therefore the transfer of pollutants. 146 147   The water for irrigation in HCMC is extracted from both the river system and the Dau Tieng Reservoir, 148   and distributed via canals. Drinking and industrial water used in the area is supplied mainly from both 149   the Dau Tieng and Tri An reservoirs and from groundwater. About 40% of the total water supply for 150   domestic and industrial use are extracted from the aquifer system (Ha, 2015; Ngo et al., 2015). The [SOURCE: Page_5]

151   aquifers that are predominantly used for groundwater resources in the study area, are the Middle- 152   Upper Pleistocene aquifers (qp^2-3) and the underlying middle Pliocene aquifer (n^22) (Ha et al., 2019b). 153 154   Climate change, sea level rise and anthropogenic activities such as urbanization and agriculture are 155   threatening the surface water quality in HCMC. In the city center the river water quality is generally 156   poor due to high concentrations of nutrients (such as NH$_4$ and PO$_4$), coliform and trace metals. In down- 157   gradient areas of river system, salinity becomes a greater issue and water cannot be used for drinking 158   (Nguyen et al., 2019). In the coastal region, groundwater resources are also affected by salinity, and 159   high Fe and Mn concentrations (Nguyen et al., 2014; Ngo et al., 2015; Ha et al., 2019b; Ha et al., 2022). 160   In comparison, in the higher-altitude area (including this study area), groundwater quality is less 161   impacted by salinity but more by acidity, high NO$_3$ and high Al concentrations (Ha et al., 2022). 162 [SOURCE: Page_6]

163 [SOURCE: Page_6]

164   3. Methods 165 166   In this study, the environmental tracers (δ18O, δ2H, major ions and 3H) are used to inform us on the 167   recharge processes in the rural area north of HCMC. The hydrochemical tracers are also used to identify 168   processes affecting pH values and NO$_3$ concentrations. Monthly field visits of groundwater and river 169   water sites were conducted for hydrochemical sampling and measurements from January 2019 to May 170   2020. Samples of rainfall isotopes were collected from 2018 to end 2019, and rainfall volumes and 171   river water level data were obtained for the period from January 2018 to August 2020. 172 173   3.1 Water levels and rainfall 174   Water levels of the Saigon River were monitored at the Thu Dau Mot station (located at 106.6531, 175   10.9494), and were accessed from the Southern Regional Hydrometeorological Center. Rainfall data 176   was also accessed from this center, which is located approximately 13 km southeast of the study area. 177   The monthly rainfall values were calculated as the sum of hourly measurements. 178 179   Hydraulic head data for 5 bores in the study area (locations shown in Fig. 1b) are from the government 180   monitored groundwater bores (Division for Water Resources Planning and Investigation for the South 181   of Vietnam; DWRPIS, 2018). The depth of bores ranges from 21.7 to 30.5 m with screen intervals of up 182   to 8m (all are screened within the aquifer qp^2-3). Data from these bores was only available from 1994 183   to 2015. 184 [SOURCE: Page_6]

185   3.2 Hydrochemistry of river water and groundwater 186   To analyse the origin of groundwater, and thereby determine the relative contributions of rainfall and 187   river recharge to the shallow groundwater system, the stable isotope (δ18O and δ2H) values of the 188   different waters were compared, and coupled with EC, major ion and 3H data. Monthly samples during 189   one wet season (2019) and two dry seasons (2019 and 2020) were collected from three river sites 190   (RW$_2$, RW$_7$ and PC) and seven groundwater sites for hydrochemical tracers (Table 1). The locations of 191   the groundwater and river water sites are presented in figure 1b, except river sampling at site PC which 192   is located 10km downstream from the study area: 10.980959°N, 106.643781°E. Many of the 193   groundwater sites were selected to represent locations in proximity to the Saigon River, a potential 194   recharge source for the shallow groundwater. Five of the groundwater sites are located between 50- 195   400 m from the Saigon River’s edge, whilst the 2 other sites (GW$_1$ and GW$_6$) are located 1.3 and 3 km 196   respectively from the river. Stable isotopes of rainfall (41 samples during the wet seasons of 2018- 197   2019) were also collected at the CARE HCMC weather station (latitude: 10.774551, longitude: 198   106.661356), which is located 30 km SSE of the study area. Collection of these rainfall samples also 199   included the analysis of EC and pH (Nguyen et al 2020). 200 201   All except one site (GW$_8$) were privately owned bores that are used for domestic (including drinking 202   water supplies) and/or agricultural purposes. The groundwater samples from these bores were 203   collected using the installed pumps, which pump groundwater from near the base of the bore. The site 204   GW$_8$ (with a screened interval of 7-11 m) is a piezometer installed during this study that is located 205   close to the Saigon River (50 m during the dry season). The depths for the groundwater bores sampled 206   range from 11-28 m, which corresponds with the depth of the shallow Upper-Middle Pleistocene (qp^2- 207   3) aquifer (Fig. 1c). One bore (GW$_5$) is screened in the deeper Lower Pleistocene aquifer (qp^1) and has [SOURCE: Page_7]

208   a depth of 40 m. Since private bores are screened along the length of the casing, the GW$_5$ samples are 209   likely a mix of the shallow and deeper aquifers. 210 211   For the hydrochemical analysis, all groundwater bores were pumped until the EC stabilised, after which 212   the field parameters were measured and samples were collected for laboratory analysis. All river 213   samples were collected at ~30 cm depth from the middle of the river. For the pH data from the 214   longitudinal profile of the Saigon River, this includes data from the study of Nguyen et al 2022 (in 215   revision). 216 217   For both river and groundwater samples the EC and temperature were measured in the field using a 218   field-calibrated WTW 3420 meter, and the pH was measured using a WTW 3310 meter. For the 219   concentrations of N species (NH$_4$, NO$_3$ and NO$_2$) samples were analysed upon return to the CARE [SOURCE: Page_7]

220   laboratories at the Ho Chi Minh City University of Technology. Samples were immediately filtered 221   through a Whatman GF/F filter (porosity 0.7 μm) for analysis of dissolved nitrogen species. These 222   parameters were analysed by standard colorimetric methods (American Public Health 223   Association:APHA, 1995). Additional water samples were collected in the field monthly for stable 224   isotopes δ18O and δ2H, major ions and tritium, with results presented in Tables 1 and 2. 225 226   The major ions and stable isotopes were analysed at the Hydrochemistry Laboratory, University of 227   Avignon, France. Samples for ion analyses were filtered in the field (0.45 µm cellulose nitrate filter) 228   and an aliquot was acidified to pH < 2 (ultrapure 16N HNO$_3$). Anions and cations were analysed using 229   ion chromatography (Dionex; ICS1100) and ion analysis uncertainty is in the order of 3 %. Stable 230   isotope values (δ2H and δ18O) were analysed using a LGR (Los Gatos Research) laser spectrometer; the 231   uncertainty is δ18O = ±0.1‰ and δ2H = ±1‰. Samples were also analysed for 3H contents (Table 2) by 232   low level liquid scintillation counting after electrolytic enrichment using a scintillation counter (Tri-Carb 233   3180 TR/SL) at the Hydrochemistry Laboratory, University of Avignon, France. The results are returned 234   to 2-sigma and the uncertainty associated with 3H results ranges from 0.2 to 0.6 TU (Table 3). 235 236   3.3 Groundwater storage calculations 237   Water table fluctuations, when measured over a seasonal-scale in shallow aquifers, represent changes 238   in groundwater storage (ΔSgw) (Healy and Cook, 2002; Koïta et al., 2018). To quantify changes in wet 239   season groundwater storage in the study area the equation (1) (Healy and Cook, 2002) was used; 240   ΔSgw = Sy.Δh                                                                         (1) 241   Where the difference in hydraulic head values (Δh) over a one year period is calculated as the change 242   in hydraulic head from the peak of the wet season to the point exponentially extrapolated from the 243   antecedent recession curve (Healy and Cook, 2002). Specific yield values (Sy) were estimated from the 244   collation presented by Johnson (1967) based on the lithological logs provided by DWRPIS for each bore 245   in the shallow qp^2-3 aquifer; medium to rough sand mixed with gravel for bores WL$_2$ and WL$_3$ (Sy = 246   0.25), and fine to medium sand for bores WL$_1$, WL$_4$ and WL$_5$ (Sy = 0.21). These values are similar with 247   the values calculated for the shallow qp^2-3 aquifer in the HCMC region, which averaged 0.26 (Vuong, 248   2010). 249 250   3.4 Geochemical modelling 251   A component mixing model was used to determine the relative recharge to the aquifer from wet 252   season rainfall. The model used to calculate wet season rainfall recharge is based on monthly time step 253   calculations of the equation (2) described in Clark and Fritz (1997). [SOURCE: Page_8]

254   Fr = (Cmix – Cgw) / (Cr – Cgw)                                                                      (2) 255   where the fraction of rainfall contribution to the groundwater aquifer (Fr) at the site is calculated using 256   the δ18O values for groundwater from the aquifer one month prior (Cgw), the δ18O values of 257   groundwater after recharge (Cmix), and the weighted δ18O values of rain water from the previous month 258   (Cr). The values used to calculate Fr and the Fr results for groundwater sites GW$_3$ and GW$_4$ are 259   presented in Table 1. 260 261 262   4. Results 263 264   4.1 Comparison of seasonal changes in rainfall and river water 265   During the sampling period for this study (Jan-18 to May-20), the seasonal rainfall peaks occurred 266   during the months of May to November in 2018 and May to October in 2019 (Fig. 2a). In comparison, 267   seasonal river level peaks are observed during October 2018 to January 2019 for the first year, and 268   during October 2019 to February 2020 for the second year (Fig. 2b). Therefore, there is a time lag in 269   the peak of the river water levels of up to 5 months after the rainfall peak. The river water levels shows 270   little correlation with rainfall due to many factors, including the regulation by the upstream Dau Tieng 271   Dam and tidal influences (Camenen et al., 2020). 272 273   4.2 Comparison of annual groundwater level fluctuations and rainfall 274   Hydraulic head data from the government monitored groundwater bores that are screened within the 275   same shallow aquifer (qp^2-3) and are located nearby the sites sampled for stable isotopes (Fig. 1b), 276   highlights significant wet season fluctuations (Fig. 3a). From 1994-2015, for the five monitoring bores 277   the groundwater fluctuations increased during the wet season on average by 2.3-4.4 m, with maximum 278   increases of 4.9-7.7 m. The groundwater hydraulic heads mostly peaked during the month of 279   November, and therefore at the end of the rainfall wet season, showing a time lag of 6 months from 280   the start of the rainfall season (typically in May). This indicates that groundwater from these sites are 281   more influenced by rainfall rather than river recharge. 282 283   The calculated values of annual changes in groundwater storage for each year of the monitoring data 284   are presented in figure 3b, and when plotted relative to the annual rainfall values this highlights that 285   rainfall is not the only influencing factor on changes in groundwater storage. When the storage and 286   rainfall data are linearly correlated the R$_2$ values range from 0.35 to 0.52. The highest correlation is at 287   site WL$_1$, which also shows the lowest increases in hydraulic heads during the wet season (average of 288   2.3 m). In comparison, sites WL$_2$ and WL$_4$ show the lowest correlation values between changes in [SOURCE: Page_9]

289   groundwater storage and rainfall, and these sites also have the highest increases in hydraulic heads 290   during the wet season (4.4 and 4.0 m respectively). Therefore, and especially at sites WL$_2$ and WL$_4$, 291   the large seasonal fluctuations in ground storage are also potentially influenced by other factors such 292   as declines in the water table due to dry season groundwater pumping, and/or lateral flowing 293   groundwater that has originated from rainfall recharge in other regions. The changes in groundwater 294   storage calculated using the water table fluctuation method over the seasonal scale represents both 295   recharge and discharge processes, and storage values are heavily influenced by changes in pressure 296   effects during these processes. Therefore, it is not possible to analyse seasonal recharge processes in 297   the study area just by using this method and the seasonal hydraulic head data. 298 299   4.3 Stable isotopes 300 301   4.3.1 Rainfall, river and groundwater 302   The stable isotope values of the rainfall samples at HCMC show a large range in values for both δ18O (- 303   12.07 to -0.98 ‰) and δ2H (-84.8 to 7.6 ‰), and the weighted annual averages for δ18O and δ2H values 304   are lower in 2018 (-6.96 and -49.0 ‰) compared with 2019 (-5.89 and -36 ‰; Figure 4a). The lowest 305   values are observed during the heavier rainfall events, and towards the end of the rainfall season (Fig. 306   2a). During 2018 and 2019, lowest isotope values were observed in November (weighted monthly 307   average δ18O = -12.07 and δ2H = -84.8 ‰) and September (weighted monthly average δ18O = -9.01 and 308   δ2H = -64.0 ‰) respectively. Lower isotope values are typical of intense wet season rainfall events for 309   this region of SE Asia (Aggaarwal et al., 2004). 310 311   River isotope values are plotted relative to the local (HCMC) meteoric water line (LMWL) calculated 312   from the 41 HCMC rain samples collected during this study (Fig. 4a). Most of the water sampled from 313   the shallow profile of the river indicate evaporation is occurring, which results in a large range in river 314   δ18O (-7.74 to -2.22 ‰) and δ2H (-52.3 to -27.3 ‰) values (Fig. 4a). The evaporation is occurring mostly 315   during the dry season months, resulting in increasing values during the months January to April (Fig. 316   2b). The Saigon River depth ranges between 5-18 m (Nguyen et al., 2019), and in 2017 samples for 317   stable isotopes in the river were collected at the surface (within 30 cm) and at the bottom of the river 318   along a longitudinal profile of 18 m. Results showed an isotopic stratification where the river water 319   was evaporated in the upper profile of the water column (-6.54 to -7.02, and -50.72 to -52.09 ‰ for 320   δ18O and δ2H respectively), and the water at the bottom of the water column was not evaporated (- 321   8.19 to -8.24, and -55.25 to -55.29 ‰ for δ18O and δ2H respectively). 322 [SOURCE: Page_10]

323   Groundwater isotope values are also plotted relative to the LMWL, and the weighted average values 324   for different rainfall event intensities (Fig. 4a). The results highlight that the groundwater values for 325   both δ18O (-7.00 to -6.45 ‰) and δ2H (-45.3 to -41.9 ‰) are closest to values of medium rainfall (100- 326   150 mm), and groundwater values also range in between the weighted average rainfall values for 2018 327   and 2019 (Fig. 4a). The groundwater isotope values close to the MWL also indicate that there is no 328   significant evaporation or mixing with evaporated waters. 329 330   4.3.2 Seasonal variations in groundwater δ18O 331   The variability in isotope values from groundwater sampled during the wet season (May to October 332   2019; rainfall levels 204-514 mm/month) compared with the dry season (November 2019 to March 333   2020; rainfall levels < 100 mm/month) is analysed for each site (Fig. 4b). The results highlight that 334   groundwater from site GW$_3$ has the highest difference in wet-dry season variations, with maximum 335   changes in wet season values up to 0.56 ‰ compared with maximum dry season changes of 0.08 ‰. 336   This is the only site where the differences in changes during the wet and dry season are above the 337   uncertainty associated with the δ18O values (0.10 ‰; Fig. 4b). 338 339   Wet season variability of groundwater δ18O values is relatively high at both sites GW$_3$ and GW$_4$ 340   (maximum wet seasons changes are 0.56 and 0.31 ‰ respectively) compared with all other sites (≤ 341   0.17), which is possibly due to differences in unsaturated and saturated zone matrix properties. Since 342   rainfall δ18O values have a high variability (for example σ = 1.93 ‰ during the 2019 wet season), these 343   results may indicate that at sites GW$_3$ and GW$_4$ there is more rapid wet season rainfall recharge 344   compared with the other sites. Relatively rapid unsaturated and saturated zone vertical flow allows us 345   to capture the variability of the rainfall δ18O values. In comparison, seasonal δ18O groundwater values 346   are likely more homogenous where there is slower (or lower) vertical flow, and therefore time for 347   mixing, and a groundwater system that is buffered from the event- or even seasonal-scale variations 348   in rainfall isotopes. 349 350   Since groundwater at sites GW$_3$ and GW$_4$ showed highest δ18O variability during the wet season, these 351   sites were selected for isotope modelling. The results of modelling the fraction of rainfall recharge 352   contribution to the aquifer at sites GW$_3$ and GW$_4$ during the wet season are presented in figure 4c. At 353   site GW$_3$ the results highlight that the estimated fraction of rain (Fr) in the groundwater varied from 354   16 % in July to 2 % in October. In comparison, at site GW$_4$, the monthly rainfall recharge contributions 355   were only up to 5 % in October. Based in these Fr results and the lower wet season changes in δ18O [SOURCE: Page_11]

356   values at GW$_4$ compared with GW$_3$ (Fig. 4b), there may be relatively lower vertical flow rates at GW$_4$ 357   compared with GW$_3$. 358 359   4.4 Chemistry of waters 360   When stable isotope values of groundwater are compared with EC values, the results show a relatively 361   high variation in EC values of groundwater that can be divided into two main groups (Fig. 5a). Group 362   one includes groundwater sites (GW$_2$, GW$_3$, GW$_4$) where the EC values are relatively low (20-55 363   µS/cm; average of 39 µS/cm) and therefore closer to rainfall values (9-30 µS/cm). In comparison, 364   Group two includes groundwater sites (GW$_1$, GW$_5$, GW$_6$, GW$_8$) with relatively high ranges in EC values 365   (75-388 µS/cm; average of 167 µS/cm), which are closer to the ranges observed for river water (all 366   Saigon River samples: 76-280 µS/cm, and where river samples are not evaporated EC < 130 µs/cm). 367 368   The relatively low EC values of Group one highlights a rainfall source for groundwater recharge. This 369   Group one includes groundwater from site GW$_3$, where the δ18O values indicate high wet season 370   changes due to rainfall recharge (Fig. 5b). The two other sites in this group, GW$_2$ and GW$_4$, are 371   therefore recharged by rainfall but at slower rates compared with GW$_3$. Groundwater from site GW$_4$ 372   shows greater wet season changes of the δ18O values compared to GW$_2$ (Fig. 5b). These results may 373   therefore indicate that the order from most rapid to slowest wet season rainfall recharge is GW$_3$, GW$_4$ 374   and GW$_2$. 375 376   The relatively high EC values of Group two (GW$_1$, GW$_5$, GW$_6$ and GW$_8$) highlight either (i) a rainfall 377   source for groundwater recharge with subsequent water-rock interaction processes increasing EC 378   values, or (ii) recharge of a surface water source (non-evaporated), since the groundwater EC are 379   similar to unevaporated Saigon River values during the months when river water levels are highest 380   (Fig. 5c). 381 382   Apart from EC values, a major distinction between the Group one and two are the SiO$_2$/Cl ratios, with 383   Group one having higher ratios (5.8-9.5) compared with Group two (0.3-2.4; Fig. 6a). These differences 384   in ratios are driven by a change in Cl rather than SiO$_2$, where Group two groundwater has higher Cl 385   concentrations compared with Group one (Table 3). Relatively high Cl ratios in Group two groundwater 386   compared with Group one may result from agricultural (fertiliser and manure) and domestic (septic 387   leakage) contamination of groundwater. There are also relatively high nitrogen concentrations (as NO$_3$) 388   associated with high Cl concentrations at some of the sites (GW$_1$ and GW$_8$: NO$_3$ up to 29.7 mg/L; Fig. 389   6b), which appear to confirm this contamination. High NO$_3$ concentrations are not the result of river [SOURCE: Page_12]

390   recharge to groundwater because the river NO$_3$ concentrations are relatively low (≤ 1.8 mg/L; Table 2). 391   However, many sites in Group two also show relatively low NO$_3$ concentrations (0.9-5.3 mg/L) within 392   a similar range to groundwater from Group one (NO$_3$ mg/L = 1.3-4.0; Fig. 6b). 393 394   Groundwater from both Group one and two have relatively high SiO$_2$ concentrations (10.5-13.8 mg 395   SiO$_2$/L, except for site GW$_6$ where SiO$_2$ < 7mg/L; Table 3). This is particularly the case for Group one 396   groundwater (GW$_2$, GW$_3$ and GW$_4$) where the SiO$_2$ concentrations are 74-80 % of the TDS values. High 397   SiO$_2$ concentrations are indicative of mineral dissolution reactions occurring during infiltration of 398   rainfall via the heavily leached acrisol soils. Infiltration via these soils can result in relatively high Si 399   concentrations relative to contributions from other ions, and low groundwater pH values (Fig. 6a). 400 401   4.5 pH of waters 402   The groundwater pH values range from 3.6-5.6 (average of 4.2; Table 1), with the lowest pH values 403   observed at site GW$_5$, and the highest at site GW$_6$. The Saigon River has higher pH values (4.8-6.1; Fig. 404   6b) compared with groundwater, with the exception of groundwater at site GW$_6$ that has similar 405   ranges in pH (4.6-5.6) to the river water (possibly due to recharge of diverted river water via the 406   irrigation canal). Average rainfall pH values (6.7 at HCMC) are also higher than groundwater values. 407   The influence of the relatively low groundwater pH values in this study area are observed in the 408   upstream longitudinal Saigon River pH values (Fig. 7a); which, as discussed below, highlights a localized 409   phenomenon driving low groundwater pH values. 410 411   The low groundwater pH values shows little correlation with dry or wet season variability or between 412   the two groups (Fig. 7b). At the site GW$_3$, where stable isotopes indicate there is relatively greater 413   rainfall recharge, the standard deviation of the pH during the wet season is low (0.07) compared with 414   values at sites GW$_4$ (0.17) and GW$_2$ (0.29). Therefore, this indicates a relatively stable groundwater 415   pH environment during periods when rainfall recharge is highest. 416 417   4.6 Tritium of waters 418   The 3H values of rainfall and river water analysed in this study ranges from 1.3-1.6 and 0.8-3.5 TU 419   respectively (Table 3). These values are consistent with recent studies that have reported rainfall 3H 420   values in Vietnam ranging on average from 2-3.4 TU (Hanoi; Anh et al., 2018) and < 2 TU (Mekong 421   Delta; Nguyen et al., 2007). Therefore, today’s atmospheric 3H values reflect natural conditions that 422   are no longer affected by the 1960s bomb pulse. 423 [SOURCE: Page_13]

424   The tritium (3H) results for groundwater are relatively low compared with local rainfall and river water 425   sampled in this study, with values from 0.5 to 1.7 TU (Table 3). These values suggest that groundwater 426   in the semi-confined or confined aquifers in the area is a mixture of recent water (< 60 years old) with 427   older water (>70 years old or prior to 1950). There are no significant differences between wet and dry 428   season averages detected at each groundwater site (Fig. 8). Similarly, the difference in average 3H 429   values between the groundwater sites is insignificant. 430 431 432   5. Discussion 433 434   5.1 Recharge processes 435   Shallow and fresh groundwater resources in the Mekong Delta are an important resource for 436   inhabitants. Low groundwater TDS can indicate areas of the Delta that receives greater volumes of 437   recent recharge and no seawater intrusion compared with the more saline (TDS > 1g/L) areas of the 438   southern Mekong Delta (Fig. 9). Across much of the Mekong Delta the Upper-Middle Pleistocene 439   aquifer system is semi-confined by the shallow clay-rich Holocene alluvial deposits, which increase in 440   thickness (and therefore effectiveness as a confining unit) from north to south. Therefore, the northern 441   Mekong Delta and surrounding edges are important areas for groundwater recharge (e.g. 442   Minderhound et al., 2000). 443 444   Transient dating tracers, such as 3H, highlight mixing between recent water (< 60 years old) with older 445   water (> 70 years old), and are therefore useful to identify regions where there is recent recharge. 446   Across the Mekong Delta, the 3H data for groundwater from the low salinity areas of the Pleistocene 447   aquifer is collated in Figure 9. Where the mean residence times of groundwater in the Pleistocene 448   aquifer indicates recent recharge and where the TDS is relatively low, this indicates important areas of 449   local recharge for the Delta. In the study area, located in the northeast of the Mekong Delta, the 450   shallow clay layers are discontinuous, which results in recent recharge. This is consistent with a more 451   regional analysis of the 3H values of groundwater undertaken by Vinh et al. (2017; Fig. 9). 452 453   Recent recharge was also observed for groundwater sampled from the underlying Lower Pleistocene 454   aquifer. Dung et al. (1992) also reported that the lowest groundwater radiocarbon mean residence 455   times in the Pliocene aquifer were observed in the northeast of the Mekong Delta (corresponding with 456   this study area). Recharge of this semi-confined deeper aquifer, may be due either to the discontinuity 457   of the clay-rich layer that normally confines the aquifer, or is the result of the installation of boreholes, [SOURCE: Page_14]

458   which has been found to induce vertical leakage between aquifers in the Mekong Delta (Ha et al., 459   2019a). 460 461   Rainfall recharge of both the Upper-Middle and Lower Pleistocene aquifers imply that this is an 462   important recharge area for aquifer systems of the Mekong Delta. In comparison, studies in the 463   northern margin of the Cambodian Mekong Delta shows varied results. In a study by Tweed et al. 464   (2020), the apparent 3H concentrations were below detection limits in groundwater from the Upper- 465   Middle Pleistocene aquifer in the Cambodian section of the Delta (Fig. 9). Whereas, a separate study 466   in the northern area of the Cambodian Mekong Delta highlighted that where the shallow clay layers 467   are incised by rivers or wetlands, the groundwater ages of the Pleistocene aquifer can be relatively 468   recent (e.g. < 60 years, Lawson et al., 2013; Richards et al., 2019; Fig. 9). 469 470   Along the Mekong groundwater basin margins, the relatively younger groundwater areas also 471   correspond to where there are fewer or thinner layers of Holocene (clay-rich) deposits, and increased 472   outcrops of the Upper-Middle Pleistocene aquifer (e.g. Ha et al., 2019b). In deltas where surface 473   sediments are dominated by clay-rich Holocene deposits, regional recharge rates are often low, and 474   therefore localized recharge processes take on an important role for groundwater resources. At the 475   regional-scale, spatial variabilities in the recharge rates of the Mekong Delta have been associated with 476   the location of rivers (e.g. Duy et al., 2021) and the thickness of the Holocene sediments (e.g. 477   Minderhound et al., 2020). In the study area, average daily water levels of the Saigon River from the 478   Thu Dau Mot monitoring station are up to 0.91 m (Fig. 2b). Also data from a previous study by Ha 479   (2015) showed that at other stations along the Saigon River the water levels are also generally less 480   than 2 m AMSL. In comparison the monitored groundwater levels from WL$_1$-WL$_4$ are all > 2 m AMSL 481   (boreholes are located 1.3-3.8 km from the river; Fig. 3a). Therefore, there is a hydraulic gradient that 482   indicates flow from the shallow aquifer at sites WL$_1$-WL$_4$ towards the river. The δ18O, δ2H and EC 483   results in this study indicate that for the groundwater sites sampled in this study, there is no recharge 484   by evaporated river water, but in close proximity to the river (e.g. site GW$_8$ ≤ 60 m from the river) 485   there is potentially seasonal recharge by unevaporated river water to the aquifer during high river 486   flows. In addition, in close proximity to irrigation channels (e.g. site GW$_6$ ≤ 20 m from the channel) 487   there is potential recharge by irrigation water. Further work is required to analyse spatial-temporal 488   dynamics of near river bank recharge to the aquifer and the extent of recharge from local irrigation 489   practices. 490 491   Stable isotopes are commonly used in catchment-scale recharge studies because they integrate small- 492   scale variability (González-Trinidad et al., 2017). In this study, the stable isotopes helped to identify the [SOURCE: Page_15]

493   spatial heterogeneity in seasonal recharge processes. For example, the calculations of the fraction of 494   recent rain in groundwater from the δ18O modelling highlighted an increased fraction of rainfall at site 495   GW$_3$ (12-16 %) compared with site GW$_4$ (≤ 5%). These values are similar or lower to vertical infiltration 496   rates (mean values 12-29% monthly rainfall) calculated from iodine-131 tracing tests in the 497   unsaturated zone, however this was undertaken in an area where the Middle Pleistocene aquifer 498   outcrops at the surface (Nguyen et al. 2013). The isotope modelled recharge values are also lower in 499   comparison to the calculations of changes in groundwater storage using the water table fluctuation 500   method in this study. For example, the annual changes in groundwater fluctuations at site WL$_1$ showed 501   the greatest correlations with rainfall compared with the other sites WL$_2$-WL$_5$ (Fig. 4b), and the 502   changes in groundwater storage at site WL$_1$ was on average 23% of rainfall (with a range in values 503   from 9-45 % over the 10-year monitoring period). These results are relatively high since the water table 504   fluctuations are also affected by dry season increases in pumping, the values are therefore an over- 505   estimation of net recharge to the aquifer. 506 507   5.2 Recharge and groundwater quality 508   Groundwater salinity and high arsenic concentrations are the most commonly discussed 509   contamination risks for the shallow groundwater aquifers of the Mekong Delta (e.g. Buschmann et al., 510   2008; Richards et al., 2017). In the NE region of the Delta, in the study area, the groundwater system 511   has both low groundwater salinity and low arsenic concentrations (< 50 µg/L; Fig. 9). However, there 512   remain other groundwater quality issues such as the (i) low groundwater pH values, which has resulted 513   in the mobilisation of trace metals, is corrosive to infrastructure and is a health risk for dependent 514   ecosystems, and (ii) additional contaminants from the land surface including sources from domestic 515   and agricultural practices (e.g. Ha et al., 2022). 516 517   The low groundwater pH results observed in this study are consistent with previous work by Nguyen 518   et al. (2014) who reported pH values ranging from 3 to 5.5 from groundwater of the Upper-Middle 519   Pleistocene aquifer located on the opposite side of the Saigon River, and by Strady et al. (2017) who 520   also observed decreases in the upper Saigon River pH values compared with downstream values. 521   Others studies by Ha (2019) and Ha et al. (2022) also found that groundwater pH < 5 (as low as 3.2) is 522   commonly observed in the high altitude area (elevation > 5 m AMSL) of southern Vietnam. In this study 523   area the infiltration of rainfall via the acrisol soils naturally results in the low groundwater EC, which is 524   a common occurrence in many tropical humid recharge areas where the soil and rocks resist 525   weathering (e.g. Knutsson, 1994). The acrisol soils of the study area are characterised as being rich in 526   stable clays (e.g. kaolinite, halloysite), quartz and Fe (hydr)oxides (e.g. goethite), poor in organic matter 527   contents, low in cations, and low in pH (Ha et al., 2019b). Due to the low contributions of cations and [SOURCE: Page_16]

528   alkalinity to groundwater in this environment, the groundwater is sensitive to acidification. A previous 529   study in the area by Ha (2019) found that only minor amounts of nitrification (ie. where NO$_3$ 530   concentrations remain < 5 mg/L) in this low alkaline groundwater results in the acidification of 531   groundwater, and subsequently mobilises trace metals, including aluminium and lead. This is distinct 532   from the processes controlling groundwater acidification along the coastline of the Saigon River Basin 533   and throughout large areas of the Mekong Delta, where pyrite oxidation plays a major role in 534   controlling the pH of groundwater (Ha et al., 2019a, b). This is also distinct from many studies 535   worldwide, where acidic groundwater has been attributed to atmospheric depositions (e.g. Hicks et al. 536   2008). The results of this study highlighted that during high local rainfall recharge of the Upper-Middle 537   Pleistocene aquifer, and therefore high vertical inflows, the pH of groundwater remains relatively low 538   and stable (site GW$_3$), compared with other sites where there is less rainfall recharge and slower 539   vertical flows (e.g. sites GW$_2$ and GW$_4$; Fig. 7b). Therefore, the nitrification reactions resulting in low 540   groundwater pH values are occurring during the infiltration of recharging rainfall, and following this 541   infiltration the pH values remain relatively stable. 542 543   Results from this study highlight that both the shallow (Upper-Middle Pleistocene) and underlying 544   (Lower Pleistocene) aquifers may not be protected from surface contaminant transfers by continuous 545   extended shallow clay layers. These clay layers do not act as effective generalized barriers of rainfall 546   infiltration, even if in some sites much slower vertical recharge is noticed. Therefore, the spatio- 547   temporal variations in NO$_3$ concentrations observed in groundwater in this study area are likely related 548   to the origins of the contaminants. The rural study area provides many opportunities for nitrate 549   contamination sources, including agricultural practices (fertilisers, animal manure) and domestic 550   wastewater (e.g. Nguyen et al. 2013; Nguyen et al 2019). At site GW$_8$, the high NO$_3$ concentrations 551   (20.4-29.7 mg/L) are not the result of river recharge, but instead are likely the result of rainfall recharge 552   transfers of agricultural fertiliser, or are the result of nitrification processes (since NH$_4$ concentrations 553   are low; 0.3-0.5 mg/L). In comparison, at site GW$_6$, the relatively high NH$_4$ concentrations (0.7-1.4 554   mg/L) compared will all other sites (< 0.7 mg/L), may indicate contamination via wastewater or animal 555   manure. EC, pH and SiO$_2$/Cl data indicate that this site is potentially recharged from irrigation water, 556   and so the contamination may be a result of natural fertiliser applications for the irrigation crop. 557   Several previous studies have also suggested that the high NO$_3$ concentrations in shallow 558   groundwaters (< 50 m depth from land’s surface) of southern Vietnam is caused by anthropogenic 559   activities, such as wastewater leakage and fertilizer application (Nguyen et al., 2013; Ha, 2019; Ha et 560   al., 2022). 561 [SOURCE: Page_17]

562   However, at most groundwater sites the NO$_3$ concentrations remain low, for example five of the seven 563   groundwater sites have NO$_3$ concentrations that remain ≤ 5.3 mg NO$_3$/L. The low NO$_3$ groundwater 564   concentrations are not due to changes in N speciation, since values for NO$_2$ and NH$_4$ also remain low 565   (Table 1). Neither are low NO$_3$ concentrations due to denitrification, since they are not associated with 566   declines in the dissolved oxygen of groundwater (Table 1), and there may be an inhibition of 567   denitrification in these groundwaters where pH is ≤ 5.6 (e.g. Huno et al., 2018). Previous studies have 568   shown that the presence of acrisol soils have resulted in decreases in NO$_3$ concentrations in soil water 569   and groundwater due to NO$_3$ adsorption (e.g. Harmand et al., 2007). Within the pH ranges for these 570   groundwaters, adsorption of NO$_3$ may be occurring with the positive surface charges of goethite 571   minerals, since the maximum surface complexation occurs at pH < 6.0 (e.g. Drever, 1997). Adsorption 572   of NO$_3$ to mineral sites may therefore result in a delay, and not a reduction, in the transfers of NO$_3$ to 573   the groundwater system. 574 575 [SOURCE: Page_18]

576   Conclusions 577   In the northeastern region of the Mekong Delta the shallow aquifers are predominantly recharged by 578   heavy (> 100 mm/month) wet season rainfall events. Both the shallow (Upper-Middle Pleistocene) and 579   underlying (Lower Pleistocene) aquifers have detectable 3H throughout both wet and dry seasons, 580   therefore they both have received recent recharge. However, the variations in seasonal fluctuations 581   of stable isotopes highlighted significant local variations in rainfall recharge rates that reflect the 582   heterogeneous distribution of sands and clays. The infiltration of rainfall recharge via the acrisol soils 583   results in low EC and alkalinity groundwater, and therefore low pH values due to nitrification processes. 584   Although there is a hydraulic connection with the Saigon River, recharge from the river to groundwater 585   may only seasonally influence shallow groundwater that is located in proximity to the river bank (< 586   60m), and via irrigation diversions. 587 588   Identifying such localized processes is important for improved comprehension and representation of 589   groundwater recharge processes. This is particularly important for the protection of recharge areas 590   from contamination, which is the major issue facing groundwater resources in wet tropical regions. 591   For the Mekong Delta, this north eastern area, where there is no salinity or arsenic issues, is a 592   potentially critical zone for groundwater resources in the Basin, and so should be protected particularly 593   in areas identified with relatively rapid recharge rates. [SOURCE: Page_18]
